Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. Then somehow the whole day is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that other people tell you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I’ll think I heard it, but then it just doesn’t stay. I’ve missed returning calls, left bills under other mail, forgotten errands I told my daughter I’d do… and I’ve realized too late I never confirmed one of my husband’s appointments.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with learning new information?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I mean… little things, maybe, but I don’t know if it’s more than anyone else my age. I haven’t really kept track.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I’m more the type to sit and think too long, if anything. I haven’t noticed myself doing things suddenly without thinking.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s actually a big part of why I came in. I can’t seem to get things in order anymore. My kitchen table is covered with appointment papers for my husband, church flyers, senior center notes, lists I started and didn’t finish… and I just sit there, not knowing what to do first. Then the day’s gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling overwhelmed or anxious about your daily tasks and responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do feel overwhelmed. It’s mostly trying to get the day in order. I’ll sit at the kitchen table with all these papers—my husband’s appointments, church things, senior center notes, lists I started—and I just don’t know which one to do first. Then somehow the whole day is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that other people tell you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I’ll think I heard it, but then it just doesn’t stay. I’ve missed returning calls, left bills under other mail, forgotten errands I told my daughter I’d do… and I’ve realized too late I never confirmed one of my husband’s appointments.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with learning new information?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed that specifically… I mean, I’m not taking classes or anything. Sometimes new instructions don’t stick right away, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty managing your daily life because of these problems with organization and memory?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s gotten in the way. I had to step back from leading a big event at the senior center because I just couldn’t keep it all straight. And my daughter… she’s been checking behind me on bills and appointments now. I don’t like that, but I suppose she doesn’t trust me to manage it all by myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. Even when I’m trying to sit still, like in a meeting, I’ll catch myself rubbing my hands or tapping my foot. Sometimes I click a pen or shift around, and then I notice people looking over.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Yes… pretty easily. If the television is on in the next room, or people start talking near me at the senior center, my mind just sort of goes there. Then I lose my place in whatever I was writing or trying to plan.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your ability to think and understand things compared to a few years ago?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big decline in understanding things. It’s more that I get mixed up or lose track, but thinking itself… I’m not sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"intent": "determine presence of significant cognitive decline", "question": "Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to make decisions or solve problems over the past few years?"}

Then the day’s gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling overwhelmed or anxious about your daily tasks and responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do feel overwhelmed. It’s mostly trying to get the day in order. I’ll sit at the kitchen table with all these papers—my husband’s appointments, church things, senior center notes, lists I started—and I just don’t know which one to do first. Then somehow the whole day is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that other people tell you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I’ll think I heard it, but then it just doesn’t stay. I’ve missed returning calls, left bills under other mail, forgotten errands I told my daughter I’d do… and I’ve realized too late I never confirmed one of my husband’s appointments.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with learning new information?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed that specifically… I mean, I’m not taking classes or anything. Sometimes new instructions don’t stick right away, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty managing your daily life because of these problems with organization and memory?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s gotten in the way. I had to step back from leading a big event at the senior center because I just couldn’t keep it all straight. And my daughter… she’s been checking behind me on bills and appointments now. I don’t like that, but I suppose she doesn’t trust me to manage it all by myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. Even when I’m trying to sit still, like in a meeting, I’ll catch myself rubbing my hands or tapping my foot. Sometimes I click a pen or shift around, and then I notice people looking over.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Yes… pretty easily. If the television is on in the next room, or people start talking near me at the senior center, my mind just sort of goes there. Then I lose my place in whatever I was writing or trying to plan.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your ability to think and understand things compared to a few years ago?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big decline in understanding things. It’s more that I get mixed up or lose track, but thinking itself… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to make decisions or solve problems over the past few years?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a major change, I don’t think… decisions can feel harder sometimes, but I can’t say it’s been a big shift over the years.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your memory and thinking abilities have gotten worse over the past few years?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say they’ve gotten worse, exactly. I notice little things, but… I can’t really tell if it’s a real change or just me having off days.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's been a noticeable decline in your cognitive abilities, such as memory or thinking, over the past few years?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I can’t say I’ve noticed a clear decline… it’s hard for me to tell.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I don’t like that, but I suppose she doesn’t trust me to manage it all by myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do. Even when I’m trying to sit still, like in a meeting, I’ll catch myself rubbing my hands or tapping my foot. Sometimes I click a pen or shift around, and then I notice people looking over.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Yes… pretty easily. If the television is on in the next room, or people start talking near me at the senior center, my mind just sort of goes there. Then I lose my place in whatever I was writing or trying to plan.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant decline in your ability to think and understand things compared to a few years ago?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a big decline in understanding things. It’s more that I get mixed up or lose track, but thinking itself… I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to make decisions or solve problems over the past few years?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a major change, I don’t think… decisions can feel harder sometimes, but I can’t say it’s been a big shift over the years.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your memory and thinking abilities have gotten worse over the past few years?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say they’ve gotten worse, exactly. I notice little things, but… I can’t really tell if it’s a real change or just me having off days.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's been a noticeable decline in your cognitive abilities, such as memory or thinking, over the past few years?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I can’t say I’ve noticed a clear decline… it’s hard for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your memory or thinking over the past few years that have affected your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way that’s really changed my daily life. I mean, I have moments, but I can’t say I’ve noticed something significant.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I can’t say I’ve noticed a clear decline… it’s hard for me to tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your memory or thinking over the past few years that have affected your daily life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way that’s really changed my daily life. I mean, I have moments, but I can’t say I’ve noticed something significant.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with memory and thinking are worse now than they were 5 years ago?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know.
